Symmetric monoidal extensions and graph cobordisms between finite sets
Andrea Bianchi

Abstract
. Given a symmetric monoidal ( ∞ , n ) (\infty,n) -category 𝒞 \mathcal{C} and a space X X , we address the problem of explicitly describing the symmetric monoidal ( ∞ , n ) (\infty,n) -category freely obtained from 𝒞 \mathcal{C} by adjoining X X new n n -morphisms with prescribed sources and targets. We develop an apparatus of tools that allow one to detect in concrete situations such a free symmetric monoidal extension. As motivating application, we introduce a symmetric monoidal ( ∞ , 2 ) (\infty,2) -category 𝔾 ​ r {\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}} of graph cobordisms between finite sets, following classical constructions of Gersten, Culler–Vogtmann and Hatcher–Vogtmann, and we exhibit it as an extension of the symmetric monoidal ( ∞ , 1 ) (\infty,1) -category Fin \mathrm{Fin} of finite sets, obtained by freely adjoining a specific list of new 1-morphisms and 2-morphisms. We recover results of Barkan–Steinebrunner and of Galatius.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification

18A40, 18B10, 18N50, 18N55, 18N65, 19D23

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Statement of results

The main goal of the article is to introduce a symmetric monoidal 2-category
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
of graph cobordisms between finite sets and to study its properties.
Roughly speaking, objects in
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
are finite sets, 1-morphisms are graph cobordisms between finite sets, and 2-morphisms are maps between graphs cobordisms obtained by collapsing trees to points. Taking disjoint unions of finite sets and graphs makes
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
into a symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,2)}
-category.
The precise construction, given in Section
3
, expands on classical constructions of Gersten
[
7
]
, Culler–Vogtmann
[
4
]
, and Hatcher–Vogtmann
[
8
]
. Most notably, the classifying space of endomorphism category
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\emptyset,\emptyset)
of the object
\emptyset\in{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
contains for all
n\geq 2
a copy of
\mathbf{B}\mathrm{Out}(F_{n})
, the classifying space of the group of outer automorphisms of a free group of rank
n
.

The main result of the article is the following “universal property”, or “presentation” for
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
, when considered as a symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,2)}
-category. For this, we introduce in Definition
3.13
the notion of
graph-like structure
on a symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,2)}
-category
\mathcal{D}
: roughly speaking, a graph-like structure is the datum of a symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,2)}
-functor
\mathrm{Fin}\to\mathcal{D}
together with the specification of a certain number of 1-morphisms and 2-morphisms in
\mathcal{D}
. Here
\mathrm{Fin}
denotes the symmetric monoidal category of finite sets and maps of finite sets, with the disjoint union monoidal structure. We shall in fact introduce in Definition
3.16
a space
\mathbf{GL}(\mathcal{D})
of graph-like structures on
\mathcal{D}
, functorially in
\mathcal{D}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}
.

Theorem A
.

The functor
\mathbf{GL}\colon\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)})\to\mathcal{S}
, associating with a symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,2)}
-category
\mathcal{D}
its space of graph-like structures, is represented by
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
, i.e. we have a natural isomorphism
\mathrm{Fun}^{\otimes}({\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}},-)^{\simeq}\simeq\mathbf{GL}
.

The universal property of the symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,2)}
-category
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
given in Theorem
A
allows us to deduce a universal property for the symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,1)}
-category
\mathrm{Gr}:=|\mathrm{Gr}|_{2}
obtained from
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
by inverting all 2-morphisms. This result, stated as Corollary
A.1
, is originally due to Barkan–Steinebrunner
[
1
]
. Compare also with
[
13
, §4.4]
, in particular with Corollary 4.22 in loc.cit.

Corollary A.1

(
[
1
]
)
.

The functor
\mathrm{CFrob}\colon\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)})\to\mathcal{S}
, associating with a symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,1)}
-category its moduli space of commutative Frobenius algebras (see Definition
2.15
), is represented by
\mathrm{Gr}
, i.e. we have a natural equivalence
\mathrm{Fun}^{\otimes}(\mathrm{Gr},-)^{\simeq}\simeq\mathrm{CFrob}
of functors
\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)})\to\mathcal{S}
.

In turn, Corollary
A.1
allows us to compute explicitly the classifying space
|\mathrm{Gr}|
of
\mathrm{Gr}
as a symmetric monoidal space. This result, stated as Corollary
A.2
, is also due to Barkan–Steinebrunner, and it is preceeded by an extremely similar result by Galatius
[
5
]
(see Remark
3.11
for more details); the way we derive Corollary
A.2
from Corollary
A.1
is also parallel to the argument of Barkan–Steinebrunner, and is only included in the present article for completeness.

Corollary A.2

(
[
1
]
)
.

The functor
\Omega\colon\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathcal{S})\to\mathcal{S}
, associating with a symmetric monoidal space its loop space at the identity element, is represented by
|\mathrm{Gr}|
, i.e. we have a natural equivalence
\mathrm{Fun}^{\otimes}(|\mathrm{Gr}|,-)^{\simeq}\simeq\Omega
of functors
\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathcal{S})\to\mathcal{S}
. Equivalently, there is an equivalence of symmetric monoidal spaces
|\mathrm{Gr}|\simeq\Omega^{\infty-1}\mathbb{S}
.

As an application of Corollary
A.1
, we construct a symmetric monoidal functor
\Re\colon\mathrm{Gr}\to\mathrm{Cospan}(\mathcal{S})
, with target the
{(\infty,1)}
-category of cospans between spaces; building on work of Culler–Vogtmann and Hatcher–Vogtmann, we prove in Theorem
8.4
that
\Re
is an
{(\infty,1)}
-subcategory inclusion and identify its image.

The universal property of
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
stated in Theorem
A
exhibits it as a free symmetric monoidal extension of
\mathrm{Fin}
, in which spaces of new 1-morphisms and 2-morphisms are adjoined. In order to prove Theorem
A
we develop an apparatus of tools to study more generally free extensions of symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,n)}
-categories, for
n\geq 1
, in which we adjoin spaces of new
n
-morphisms.

The main tools are given by the following results, contained in Sections
4
and
5
.
The setting is the following: we freely extend a symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,n)}
-category
\mathcal{C}
to a symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,n)}
-category
\mathcal{D}
by adjoining a space
X
of new
n
-morphisms of prescribed sources and targets; we further assume that there is an
\mathbb{N}
-valued grading on
n
-morphisms of
\mathcal{D}
which is additive under composition and monoidal product, such that the
\mathbb{N}
-grading vanishes on
\mathbb{N}
-morphisms coming from
\mathcal{C}
and takes values 1 on
n
-morphisms coming from
X
.

The first result tells us that, under suitable grading conditions, adjoining new
n
-morphisms to a symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,n)}
-category will not affect its objects nor its
i
-morphisms for any
i<n
.

Theorem B

(Corollary
4.23
)
.

The canonical symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,n)}
-functor
\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
restricts to an equivalence
\mathcal{C}^{n\simeq}\to\mathcal{D}^{n\simeq}
on
n
-groupoid cores.

The second result compares morphism spaces in
\mathcal{D}
and in an iterated extension of
\mathcal{C}
obtained by adjoining several copies of the new
n
-morphisms.

Theorem C

(Proposition
4.30
)
.

Let
S\in\mathrm{Fin}
and let
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
be the symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,n)}
-category obtained from
\mathcal{C}
by adjoining
S\times X
new
n
-morphisms with sources and targets prescribed as for the extension
\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
. Observe that morphisms in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
come with an
\mathbb{N}^{S}
-valued grading. Let
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
be
(n-1)
-morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
sharing all levels of sources and targets, let
Y
denote the space of
n
-morphisms from
y_{1}
to
y_{2}
in
\mathcal{D}
of
\mathbb{N}
-grading
\#S\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
Y^{\prime}
denote the space of
n
-morphisms from
y_{1}
to
y_{2}
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{N}^{S}
-grading
1^{S}
. Then the map
Y^{\prime}/\mathfrak{S}_{S}\to Y
induced by the fold symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,n)}
-functor
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{D}
is a homotopy retraction, i.e. it admits a section up to homotopy.

We actually conjecture that the map
Y^{\prime}/\mathfrak{S}_{S}\to Y
in Theorem
C
is an equivalence, see Conjecture
4.35
for more details. Theorems
B
and
C
allow one in concrete situations to restrict the study of the extension
\mathcal{D}
to understanding its spaces of
n
-morphisms of
\mathbb{N}
-grading 1. We are thus led to introduce the
\infty
-category
\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,n)}^{01})
of
01
-truncated symmetric monoidal
n
-categories
: roughly these are symmetric monoidal
n
-categories in which
n
-morphisms carry grading 0 or 1, and in which composition and monoidal product of
n
-morphisms is only defined if the sum of the gradings does not exceed 1.

Theorem D

(Corollary
5.38
)
.

There is a pullback square of large
\infty
-categories

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,n)}^{01})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,n)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathscr{P}^{01})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathscr{P}).}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\dot{\mathcal{T}}^{n},\dot{\lambda}^{n})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\iota}^{*}}

Here
\mathscr{P}\to\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)}
denotes the bicartesian fibration corresponding to the functor associating with a small category its category of space-valued presheaves, and
\mathscr{P}^{01}
is similarly defined, using presheaves with values in the category of “
01
-graded spaces”, i.e. spaces with a map to the set
\{0,1\}
. The functor
\hat{\iota}^{*}
forgets the part of grading 1 of a
01
-graded presheaf. The symmetric monoidal functor
(\dot{\mathcal{T}}^{n},\dot{\lambda}^{n})

associates with
\mathcal{E}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,n)}
the “natural”
{(\infty,1)}
-category
\dot{\mathcal{T}}^{n}(\mathcal{E})
on which the presheaf
\dot{\lambda}^{n}(\mathcal{E})
, packaging the information about
n
-morphisms in
\mathcal{E}
, is defined.

The precise definition of the functor
\dot{\mathcal{T}}^{n}
is given in Section
5
; for
n=1
we have
\dot{\mathcal{T}}^{1}(\mathcal{E})=\mathcal{E}\times\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{op}}
, over which the presheaf
\dot{\lambda}(\mathcal{E})=\mathcal{E}(-,-)
is defined. Theorem
D
allows one to compute spaces of
n
-morphisms of
\mathbb{N}
-grading 1 in
\mathcal{D}
via suitable Day convolutions of presheaves over
\dot{\mathcal{T}}^{n}(\mathcal{C})
.

We hope that this apparatus of tools can be useful in other contexts than the one presented in this article.

1.2.
Organisation of the article

In Section
2
we introduce some preliminary material, which serves as a reference. Section
3
gives a careful and elementary construction of
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
as a plain 2-category (i.e. an
{(\infty,2)}
-category with discrete spaces of 2-morphisms), as well as a description of the functor of graph-like structures on symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,2)}
-categories. Sections
4
and
5
give the apparatus of tools to study free extensions of symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,n)}
-categories for any
n\geq 1
. Section
7
is devoted to the proof of Theorem
A
. Finally, in Section
8
we prove Corollaries
A.1
and
A.2
as well as Theorem
8.4
.

1.3.
Motivation

In the forthcoming work on string topology

[
2
]
, we are going to apply Corollaries
A.1
and
A.2
to construct string operations connecting the homology groups of the mapping spaces
\mathrm{map}(X,M)
, for varying space
X\in\mathcal{S}
and for a fixed oriented Poincaré duality space
M
. We originally intended to apply directly Theorem
A
to that purpose, and this led us to the quest for a universal property for
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
and to the study of symmetric monoidal extensions of
{(\infty,n)}
-categories. We later understood, through conversations with Jan Steinebrunner, that the work of Barkan–Steinebrunner would actually suffice for the string topology applications. Since we do not have any direct application of Theorem
A
in mind, that does not pass through Corollaries
A.1
and
A.2
, we declare the following twofold motivation for this article:

•

initiate a systematic study of symmetric monoidal extensions of
{(\infty,n)}
-categories;

•

give an alternative proof of the results of Barkan–Steinebrunner.

2.
Preliminaries

In this section we recollect most of the background material that we will use throughout the article. We encourage the reader to proceed directly to the next section on a first reading of the article, and to use this section as a reference.

We fix two nested Grothendieck universes
\mathfrak{U}_{1}\subsetneq\mathfrak{U}_{2}
, and we refer to objects whose size is bounded above by
\mathfrak{U}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{U}_{2}
as “small” and “large”, respectively.
An
\infty
-category is
presentable
if it is
\mathfrak{U}_{2}
-small, admits
\mathfrak{U}_{1}
-small colimits, and is
\kappa
-accessible for some regular cardinal
\kappa<\mathfrak{U}_{1}
.

2.1.
Finite sets and simplices

We denote by
\mathcal{S}
the presentable
\infty
-category of small
\infty
-groupoids, which we refer to as “spaces”. We say that a map of spaces
X\to Y
is an
inclusion
if it exhibits
X
as equivalent to a union of connected components of
Y
; we also say that
X
is a
subspace
of
Y
.

We denote by
\mathrm{Fin}
the category of finite sets, and by
\mathrm{Fin}^{\mathrm{inj}}\subset\mathrm{Fin}
the subcategory of finite sets and injections. We regard
\mathrm{Fin}
as a full
\infty
-subcategory of
\mathcal{S}
.
For
k\geq 0
we denote by
\underline{k}\in\mathrm{Fin}
the finite set
\left\{1,\dots,k\right\}
. We have
\emptyset=\underline{0}
.

We denote by
\mathbf{\Delta}
the category of linearly ordered non-empty finite sets and weakly increasing functions; for
k\geq 0
we let
[k]\in\mathbf{\Delta}
be the object
\left\{0<\dots<k\right\}
; for
k\geq 1
and
0\leq i\leq k
we denote by
d^{i}\colon[k-1]\to[k]
the unique injective map whose image does not contain
i
; for
k\geq 0
and
0\leq i\leq k
we let
s^{i}\colon[k+1]\to[k]
be the unique surjective map whose fibre over
i
has two points. For an
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
we denote by
d_{i},s_{i}\colon\mathrm{Fun}([k],\mathcal{C})\to\mathrm{Fun}([k\pm 1],\mathcal{C})
the functors induced by
d^{i},s^{i}
.

A morphism
[k]\to[k^{\prime}]
in
\mathbf{\Delta}
is
inert
if it is injective and its image is a subsegment
\left\{i<\dots<i+k\right\}\subseteq[k^{\prime}]
, and it is
active
if its image contains
0
and
k^{\prime}
. Every morphism in
\mathbf{\Delta}
factors uniquely, up to unique isomorphism, as the composite of an active and an inert morphism. For
n\geq 1
, a morphism in
\mathbf{\Delta}^{n}
or in
(\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}})^{n}
is inert/active if each of its coordinates is (the opposite of) an inert/active morphism.
We let
\mathbf{\Delta}_{\mathrm{int}}
denote the wide subcategory of
\mathbf{\Delta}
spanned by inert morphisms.

For
n\geq 0
and a sequence
\mathbf{k}=(k_{1},\dots,k_{n})\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
we let
[\mathbf{k}]
denote the object
([k_{1}],\dots,[k_{n}])\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{n}
, and
\underline{\mathbf{k}}=\underline{k}_{1}\times\dots\times\underline{k}_{n}\in\mathrm{Fin}
.

For an
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
and for
n\geq 0
we denote by
s^{n}\mathcal{C}:=\mathrm{Fun}((\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}})^{n},\mathcal{C})
the category of
n
-fold simplicial objects in
\mathcal{C}
. We write
s\mathcal{C}=s^{1}\mathcal{C}
and identify
\mathcal{C}=s^{0}\mathcal{C}
.

For
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{S}
and
\mathbf{k}=(k_{1},\dots,k_{n})
, we let
\Delta^{\mathbf{k}}:=\mathbf{\Delta}^{n}(-,[\mathbf{k}])\in s^{n}\mathcal{S}
denote the functor represented by
[\mathbf{k}]
. For a map
f\colon[\mathbf{k}]\to[\mathbf{k}^{\prime}]
in
\mathbf{\Delta}^{n}
we also write
f\colon\Delta^{\mathbf{k}}\to\Delta^{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
for the induced map of
n
-fold simplicial spaces.

Notation 2.1
.

We denote by
E^{1}\in s\mathcal{S}
the quotient of
\Delta^{3}\in s\mathcal{S}
in which we collapse the two copies of
\Delta^{1}\subset\Delta^{3}
corresponding to the two inclusions
[1]\hookrightarrow[3]
with images
\left\{0,2\right\}
and
\left\{2,4\right\}
, respectively, to two distinct points. In symbols

E^{1}=\Delta^{3}\sqcup_{(\Delta^{1}\sqcup\Delta^{1})}(\Delta^{0}\sqcup\Delta^{0}).

Definition 2.2
.

For an
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
, an object
x\in\mathcal{C}
and a morphism
f\colon y\to z
in
\mathcal{C}
, we say that
x
is
local
with respect to
f
if the map of spaces
\mathcal{C}(y,x)\to\mathcal{C}(z,x)
induced by precomposition with
f
is an equivalence.

2.2.
Categories of categories

For
n\geq 0
we let
\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,n)}
denote the
\infty
-category of small
{(\infty,n)}
-categories.
We refer to the explicit model of
\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,n)}
as the full and reflective sub-
\infty
-category of
s^{n}\mathcal{S}
spanned by complete Segal objects, i.e. objects
X\in s^{n}\mathcal{S}
satisfying the following conditions introduced by Rezk
[
12
]
.

•

(Segal condition) The composite functor
X_{\mathrm{int}}\colon(\mathbf{\Delta}_{\mathrm{int}}^{\mathrm{op}})^{n}\hookrightarrow(\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}})^{n}\overset{X}{\to}\mathcal{S}
is right Kan extended from the full subcategory
(\mathbf{\Delta}_{\mathrm{int},\leq 1}^{\mathrm{op}})^{n}\subset(\mathbf{\Delta}_{\mathrm{int}}^{\mathrm{op}})^{n}
spanned by objects
[\mathbf{k}]
with
k_{i}\leq 1
for all
1\leq i\leq n
. This can be equivalently expressed by asking
X
to be local with respect to all of the following “spine inclusions”, for varying
\mathbf{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
:

\mathrm{colim}_{([\mathbf{k}^{\prime}]\to[\mathbf{k}])\in(\mathbf{\Delta}_{\mathrm{int}}^{n})_{/[\mathbf{k}]}}\Delta^{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\to\Delta^{\mathbf{k}}.

•

(Constancy condition) For all
1\leq i\leq n-1
, for all
\mathbf{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{i-1}
and for all maps
[\mathbf{k}^{\prime}]\to[\mathbf{k}^{\prime\prime}]
in
(\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}})^{n-i}
, the induced map
X([\mathbf{k},0,\mathbf{k}^{\prime}])\to X([\mathbf{k},0,\mathbf{k}^{\prime\prime}])
is an equivalence of spaces. Equivalently, we can ask
X
to be local with respect to the maps
\Delta^{\mathbf{k}}\boxtimes\Delta^{0}\boxtimes\Delta^{\mathbf{k}^{\prime\prime}}\to\Delta^{\mathbf{k}}\boxtimes\Delta^{0}\boxtimes\Delta^{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
, where
\boxtimes
denotes the exterior product of multisimplicial spaces (see Subsection
2.4
).

•

(Completeness condition) For all
1\leq i\leq n
and for all
\mathbf{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{i-1}
,
X
is
local
with respect to the map
\Delta^{\mathbf{k}}\boxtimes E^{1}\boxtimes\Delta^{0^{n-i}}\to\Delta^{\mathbf{k}}\boxtimes\Delta^{0}\boxtimes\Delta^{0^{n-i}}
induced by the unique map
E^{1}\to\Delta^{0}
in
s\mathcal{S}
.

Remark 2.3
.

The description of
\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,n)}\subset s^{n}\mathcal{S}
as the full subcategory on objects that are local with respect to a
set
of morphisms in
s^{n}\mathcal{S}
ensures that
\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,n)}
is an accessible localisation of
s^{n}\mathcal{S}
, and in particular
\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,n)}
is presentable.

Notation 2.4
.

For
n\geq 0
and
1\leq i\leq 2
we denote by
\mathcal{S}^{\mathfrak{U}_{i}}
and
\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,n)}^{\mathfrak{U}_{i}}
the
\infty
-category of
\mathfrak{U}_{i}
-small
\infty
-groupoids and
{(\infty,n)}
-categories, respectively; for
i=1
we omit the exponent “
\mathfrak{U}_{1}
” from the notation.

Example 2.5
.

Both
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,n)}
belong to
\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)}^{\mathfrak{U}_{2}}
and are presentable.

Spaces are the same as
(\infty,0)
-categories; for all
n\geq 1
,
(\infty,n-1)
-categories are also considered as
{(\infty,n)}
-categories with only invertible
n
-morphisms. From the point of view of
(n-1)
-fold and
n
-fold simplicial spaces, the fully faithful inclusion
\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,n-1)}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,n)}
corresponds to the restriction on
\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,n-1)}
of the functor
s^{n-1}\mathcal{S}\to s^{n}\mathcal{S}
given by left Kan extension along the inclusion
-\times[0]\colon(\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}})^{n-1}\to(\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}})^{n}
; since
[0]
is initial in
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}
, we can identify the same functor
s^{n-1}\mathcal{S}\to s^{n}\mathcal{S}
as the one given by restriction along the projection
(\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}})^{n}\to(\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}})^{n-1}
away from the last factor.
The above shows that for
n\geq 1
, the fully faithful inclusion
\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,n-1)}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,n)}
has a left adjoint
|-|_{n}\colon\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,n)}\to\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,n-1)}
, given by left Kan extension along the projection
(\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}})^{n}\to(\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}})^{n-1}
, and a right adjoint
(-)^{n\simeq}\colon\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,n)}\to\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,n-1)}
, given by restriction along
-\times[0]
; we say that
\mathcal{C}^{n\simeq}
is the
n
-groupoid core
of
\mathcal{C}
, and we regard
\mathcal{C}^{n\simeq}
as a (non-full) sub-
{(\infty,n)}
-category of
\mathcal{C}
via the counit of the adjunction. For each
n\geq 1
we also denote by
|-|,(-)^{\simeq}\colon\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,n)}\to\mathcal{S}
the left and right adjoints of the fully faithful inclusion
\mathcal{S}\to\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,n)}
given by taking constant functors out of
(\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}})^{n}
.

For
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,n)}
we denote by
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})^{\simeq}\in\mathcal{S}
the morphism space from
\mathcal{C}
to
\mathcal{D}
in
\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,n)}
; we put the symbol “
\simeq
” in order to avoid confusion with other notions of
{(\infty,n)}
-categories of
{(\infty,n)}
-functors from
\mathcal{C}
to
\mathcal{D}
appearing in the literature.

Definition 2.6
.

We define
\Theta_{0}=\left\{s_{0}\right\}
and
\mathrm{O}_{0}=\left\{s_{0},t_{0}\right\}
as the discrete groupoids with one, respectively two non-equivalent objects. For
n\geq 1
, we recursively define
\Theta_{n}
as the
n
-category with two non-equivalent objects
s_{0},t_{0}
having
\Theta_{n-1}
as
(n-1)
-category of morphisms from
s_{0}
to
t_{0}
, and otherwise having no other non-identity
i
-morphisms for any
1\leq i\leq n
. We similarly define
\mathrm{O}_{n}
, replacing “
\Theta_{n-1}
” by “
\mathrm{O}_{n-1}
” in the recursive definition of
\Theta_{n}
. Equivalently, for
n\geq 1
, we may define
\mathrm{O}_{n}
as the
{(\infty,n)}
-category
\Theta_{n+1}^{(n+1)\!\simeq}
, in particular we have an inclusion
\mathrm{O}_{n}\hookrightarrow\Theta_{n+1}
.

Example 2.7
.

The category
\mathrm{O}_{2}
has two distinct objects
s,t
and two distinct morphisms
f,g\colon s\to t
; the 2-category
\Theta_{2}
is obtained from
\mathrm{O}_{2}
by adjoining a 2-morphism
f\Rightarrow g
.

For
0\leq m\leq n
and an
{(\infty,n)}
-category
\mathcal{C}
, we identify
\mathrm{Fun}(\Theta_{m},\mathcal{C})^{\simeq}
with the space of all
m
-morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
, that is the value at
[1]^{m}\times[0]^{n-m}\in(\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}})^{n}
of a complete Segal
n
-fold simplicial space
(\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}})^{n}\to\mathcal{S}
representing
\mathcal{C}
.
Similarly,
\mathrm{O}_{m-1}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,n)}
corepresents the space of all choices of two
m-1
-morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
sharing all levels of sources and targets.

The word “
n
-category” is used for
{(\infty,n)}
-categories
\mathcal{C}
such that the restriction map
\mathrm{Fun}(\Theta_{n},\mathcal{C})^{\simeq}\to\mathrm{Fun}(\mathrm{O}_{n-1},\mathcal{C})^{\simeq}
has discrete fibres; informally, we are requiring that all spaces of
n
-morphisms between any two
(n-1)
-morphisms sharing all levels of sources and targets is discrete. We abbreviate “category” for “1-category” and “set” for “0-category”.

For
n\geq 1
, an
{(\infty,n)}
-category
\mathcal{C}
and objects
x,y,z\in\mathcal{C}
we usually denote by
\mathcal{C}(x,y)
the
(\infty,n-1)
-category of morphisms between
x
and
y
. We denote by
-\circ_{\mathrm{h}}-\colon\mathcal{C}(y,z)\times\mathcal{C}(x,y)\to\mathcal{C}(y,z)
the horizontal composition functor (for
n=1
we just write
-\circ-
).

2.3.
Symmetric monoidal categories and deloopings

For
n\geq 0
we denote by
\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,n)})
the presentable
\infty
-category of small symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,n)}
-categories, with morphisms given by symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,n)}
-functors. This can be equivalently defined as the category of commutative monoid objects
[
10
, Definition 2.4.2.1]
, or of algebras over the
E_{\infty}
-operad, in
\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,n)}
, see
[
10
, Proposition 2.4.2.5]
.
We usually denote by
\mathbf{1}\in\mathcal{C}
the monoidal unit and we denote by
-\otimes-\colon\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
the monoidal product.
When the monoidal structure on
\mathcal{C}
comes from categorical coproduct, either on
\mathcal{C}
or on a closely related
{(\infty,n)}
-category, we will instead use the notations
\emptyset
and
-\sqcup-
for the monoidal unit and the monoidal product of
\mathcal{C}
.
For small symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,n)}
-categories
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
, we let
\mathrm{Fun}^{\otimes}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}^{\prime})^{\simeq}\in\mathcal{S}
denote the corresponding morphism space in
\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,n)})
.

We usually endow
\mathrm{Fin}
and
\mathcal{S}
with the coproduct symmetric monoidal structure. When
\mathcal{S}
is endowed with the product symmetric monoidal structure, we will stress this by writing
(\mathcal{S},\times)
.

For
\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,n)})
we let
\mathbf{B}\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,n+1)})
denote the
first delooping
of
\mathcal{C}
, i.e. the essentially unique symmetric monoidal
(\infty,n+1)
-category with

\mathbf{B}\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf{B}\mathcal{C}},\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf{B}\mathcal{C}})\simeq\mathcal{C}
and such that
(\mathbf{B}\mathcal{C})^{\simeq}
is a connected space. Taking the first delooping gives a fully faithful inclusion
\mathbf{B}\colon\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,n)})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,n+1)})
with essential image given by the symmetric monoidal
(\infty,n+1)
-categories whose space of objects is connected.
We usually denote by
*=\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf{B}\mathcal{C}}
the monoidal unit of a first delooping, which we regard as a “base object”.

Example 2.8
.

,

Let
M\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathcal{S})
be a
group-like
symmetric monoidal space, i.e. the discrete monoid
\pi_{0}(M)
is an abelian group. Then for
n\geq 1
we have that
\mathbf{B}^{n}M
is the essentially constant
n
-fold simplicial space with value the essentially unique pointed
(n-1)
-connected space
X
with
\Omega^{n}X\simeq M
. By abuse of notation, we refer to this space
X
also as
\mathbf{B}^{n}M
in this case.

Definition 2.9
.

A discrete abelian monoid
M
is
ungroup-like
if the neutral element
0\in M
is the only invertible element.

We remark that all finitely generated, free abelian monoids are ungroup-like.

Example 2.10
.

Let
M
be an ungroup-like abelian monoid, considered as an object in
\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathcal{S})
. Then for
n\geq 1
the underlying
n
-fold simplicial space of
\mathbf{B}^{n}M
is the levelwise discrete
n
-fold simplicial abelian monoid
[\mathbf{k}]\mapsto M^{\underline{\mathbf{k}}}
, with face maps given by addition and degeneracy maps given by inserting
0\in M
. Note that, for a generic discrete monoid
M
, the
n
-fold simplicial set
[\mathbf{k}]\mapsto M^{\underline{\mathbf{k}}}
satisfies the Segal and constancy conditions; the ungroup-like assumption also ensures completeness.

Notation 2.11
.

In the setting of Example
2.10
, for
\nu\in\mathbf{B}^{n}M([\mathbf{k}])\simeq M^{\underline{\mathbf{k}}}
, we define the
total grading
of
x
as the sum of the coordinates of
\nu
, taken in
M
.

2.4.
Presheaves and Day convolution

For
\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)}
and for any
\infty
-category
\mathcal{D}
we denote by
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C};\mathcal{D}):=\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}};\mathcal{D})
the
\infty
-category of
\mathcal{D}
-valued presheaves over
\mathcal{C}
. If
\mathcal{D}
is endowed with a symmetric monoidal structure, we define for
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)}
an exterior product
-\boxtimes-\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C};\mathcal{D})\times\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C};\mathcal{D}^{\prime})\to\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}^{\prime};\mathcal{D})
, sending
(A,B)\mapsto A\boxtimes B
with
(A\boxtimes B)(c,c^{\prime})=A(c)\otimes B(c^{\prime})
.

If
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{S}
we just write
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C})
for
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{S})
, which is a presentable
\infty
-category.
We denote by
\!\text{\char 135\relax}\!\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C})
the Yoneda embedding. For
\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)})
, the category
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C})
is endowed with a symmetric monoidal structure
-\otimes_{\mathrm{Day}}-
given by
Day convolution
, which can be characterised by the following properties
[
10
, Corollary 4.8.1.12]
:

•

the Yoneda embedding can be promoted to a symmetric monoidal functor;

•

the monoidal product
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C})^{2}\to\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C})
preserves colimits separately in each variable.

The two properties give rise to the following formula: if
A\simeq\mathrm{colim}_{i\in I}\!\text{\char 135\relax}\!(x_{i})
and
B\simeq\mathrm{colim}_{j\in J}\!\text{\char 135\relax}\!(x^{\prime}_{j})
are objects in
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C})
, written as colimits of representable presheaves, then
A\otimes_{\mathrm{Day}}B\simeq\mathrm{colim}_{(i,j)\in I\times J}\!\text{\char 135\relax}\!(x_{i}\otimes x^{\prime}_{j})
.

A functor
F\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
induces a functor
F^{*}\colon\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})\to\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C})
by restriction, with left adjoint
F_{!}\colon\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
given by left Kan extension. The functor
F_{!}
is symmetric monoidal, whereas
F^{*}
is in general only lax symmetric monoidal.

We denote by
\mathscr{P}\to\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)}
a cartesian fibration corresponding to the functor
\mathscr{P}(-)\colon(\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)}^{\mathfrak{U}_{2}}
sending
\mathcal{C}\mapsto\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C})
and sending
F\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
to
F^{*}
. Roughly speaking, an object in
\mathscr{P}
is a pair
(\mathcal{C},A)
with
\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)}
and
A\in\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C})
. Exterior product of presheaves, sending
(A,B)\in\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C})\times\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
to the presheaf
A\boxtimes B\in\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{D})
, makes
\mathscr{P}(-)
into a lax symmetric monoidal functor.
By
[
11
, Theorem B, Remark 0.1]
we have a symmetric monoidal structure on
\mathscr{P}
as well as on the projection functor
\mathscr{P}\to\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)}
.

We can also regard
\mathscr{P}
as the full subcategory of
\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)})
spanned by right fibrations, recalling that the datum of
A\in\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C})
is equivalent to that of a right fibration over
\mathcal{C}
; in this light the functor
\mathscr{P}\to\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)}
is the restriction of the target functor
d_{0}\colon\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)})\to\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)}
, and the symmetric monoidal structure on
\mathscr{P}
is restricted from the pointwise one on
\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)})
; in particular
\mathscr{P}
has the
cartesian
symmetric monoidal structure.

2.5.
Twisted arrow categories

We denote by
i_{\mathbf{\Delta}}\colon\mathbf{\Delta}\to\mathbf{\Delta}
the non-trivial involution of
\mathbf{\Delta}
, by
\Delta_{\mathbf{\Delta}}\colon\mathbf{\Delta}\to\mathbf{\Delta}\times\mathbf{\Delta}
the diagonal functor, by
p_{\mathbf{\Delta},1},p_{\mathbf{\Delta},2}\colon\mathbf{\Delta}\times\mathbf{\Delta}\to\mathbf{\Delta}
the two projections, and by
c_{\mathbf{\Delta}}\colon\mathbf{\Delta}\times\mathbf{\Delta}\to\mathbf{\Delta}
the concatenation functor, sending
([k],[k^{\prime}])
to the lexicographic concatenation of finite ordered sets
[k]\sqcup_{<}[k^{\prime}]\simeq[k+k^{\prime}+1]
. For
j=1,2
there is a natural transformation
\eta_{\mathbf{\Delta},j}\colon p_{\mathbf{\Delta}_{j}}\Rightarrow c_{\mathbf{\Delta}}
, sending
([k_{1}],[k_{2}])
to the minimal (
j=1
) or maximal (
j=2
) inclusion
[k_{j}]\hookrightarrow[k_{1}+k_{2}+1]
.

For
X\in s\mathcal{S}=\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathcal{S})
we denote by
X^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Tw}(X)\in s\mathcal{S}
the functors
X\circ i^{\mathrm{op}}_{\mathbf{\Delta}}
and
X\circ c_{\mathbf{\Delta}}^{\mathrm{op}}\circ(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathbf{\Delta}}\times i_{\mathbf{\Delta}})^{\mathrm{op}}\circ\Delta_{\mathbf{\Delta}}^{\mathrm{op}}
. The natural transformations
\eta_{\mathbf{\Delta},1},\eta_{\mathbf{\Delta},2}
give rise to a map of simplicial spaces
\mathrm{Tw}(X)\to X\times X^{\mathrm{op}}
.

If
\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)}
, then also
\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Tw}(\mathcal{C})\in\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)}
, and the projection
\mathrm{Tw}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}
is a right fibration, corresponding to a presheaf which we denote by
\mathcal{C}(-,-)\in\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}})
, as its value on
(x,y)
is the morphism space
\mathcal{C}(x,y)
. We refer to
\mathrm{Tw}(\mathcal{C})
as the
twisted arrow category
of
\mathcal{C}
, and we denote by
d_{1}\colon\mathrm{Tw}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{C}
and
d_{0}\colon\mathrm{Tw}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}
the “source” and “target” functors, even though they are not directly related to the morphisms
d^{0},d^{1}\colon[0]\to[1]
in
\mathbf{\Delta}
.

Under the equivalence
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}})\simeq\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C};\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C}))
, the presheaf
\mathcal{C}(-,-)
corresponds in turn to
\!\text{\char 135\relax}\!\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C})
.

2.6.
Commutative Frobenius algebras

The following material can be found in
[
10
, Subsection 4.6.5]
.

Definition 2.12
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,1)}
-category, and let
x\in\mathcal{C}
be an object. A
duality datum
for
x
is the pair
(x^{\vee},c)
of an object
x^{\vee}\in\mathcal{C}
and a morphism
c\colon x\otimes x^{\vee}\to\mathbf{1}
satisfying the following property: there exists a morphism
u\colon 1\to x^{\vee}\otimes x
for which there exist homotopies
\mathrm{Id}_{x}\simeq(c\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{x})(\mathrm{Id}_{x}\otimes u)
and
\mathrm{Id}_{x^{\vee}}\simeq(\mathrm{Id}_{x^{\vee}}\otimes c)(u\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{x^{\vee}})
. In this case we say that
x^{\vee}
is a dual of
x
, and that
x
is dualisable; the maps
c
and
u
are called counit and unit of the duality.

Notation 2.13
.

Following
[
10
, Notation 4.6.1.8]
, for
\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)})
we let
\mathrm{DDat}(\mathcal{C})
denote the moduli space of duality data in
\mathcal{C}
: it is introduced as a certain full subcategory of the limit in
\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)}
of the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C};}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\otimes-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}

more precisely, we take the full subcategory spanned by objects of the form
(x,x^{\vee},c)
such that
(x^{\vee},c)
is a duality datum for
x
. We have that
\mathrm{DDat}(\mathcal{C})
is an
\infty
-groupoid, and the forgetful functor
\mathrm{DDat}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{C}^{\simeq}
sending
(x,x^{\vee},c)\mapsto x
is fully faithful with image the subspace of dualisable objects in
\mathcal{C}^{\simeq}
, see
[
10
, Lemma 4.6.1.10]
.

Lemma 2.14

(
[
10
, Lemma 4.6.1.6]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)})
and let
(x^{\vee},c)
be a duality datum for
x\in\mathcal{C}
; then for all objects
y,z\in\mathcal{C}
the composite map of spaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}(y,z\otimes x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}(y\otimes x^{\vee},z\otimes x\otimes x^{\vee})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}(y\otimes x^{\vee},z)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\otimes x^{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}(y\otimes x^{\vee},z\otimes c)}

is an equivalence.

The following is a symmetric monoidal adaptation of
[
10
, Definition 4.6.5.1]
.

Definition 2.15
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,1)}
-category.
A
commutative Frobenius algebra
in
\mathcal{C}
is the datum
(\mathfrak{I},\mathfrak{t})
of a symmetric monoidal functor
\mathfrak{I}\colon\mathrm{Fin}\to\mathcal{C}
and a morphism
\mathfrak{t}\colon\mathfrak{I}(\underline{1})\to\mathfrak{I}(\underline{0})
such that
(\mathfrak{I}(\underline{1}),\mathfrak{t}\circ\mathfrak{I}(\mu))
is a duality datum for
\mathfrak{I}(\underline{1})
, see Notation
3.12
.
We denote by
\mathrm{CFrob}\colon\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)})\to\mathcal{S}
the functor associating with a symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,1)}
-category
\mathcal{C}
its moduli space of commutative Frobenius algebras. It is the pullback in
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)}),\mathcal{S})
of the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}^{\otimes}(\mathrm{Fin},-)^{\simeq}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{DDat}(\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathcal{C})}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}

2.7.
Localisation of categories

Definition 2.16
.

We denote by
\mathrm{Rel}\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)}\subset\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)})
the full sub-
\infty
-category spanned by functors
W\to\mathcal{C}
such that
W([0])\overset{\simeq}{\to}\mathcal{C}([0])
, and such that the map
W([1])\to\mathcal{C}([1])
exhibits
W([1])
as a union of components of
\mathcal{C}([1])
; in few words,
W
is a wide subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
. An object in
\mathrm{Rel}\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)}
is usually denoted as a pair
(\mathcal{C},W)
, and is called a
relative
{(\infty,1)}
-category
. We endow
\mathrm{Rel}\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)}
with the (pointwise) product symmetric monoidal structure.

The fully faithful inclusion
\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Rel}\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)}
sending
\mathcal{C}\mapsto(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}^{\simeq})
is symmetric monoidal, and it admits a symmetric monoidal left adjoint
L\colon\mathrm{Rel}\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)}\to\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)}
, sending
(\mathcal{C},W)
to the localisation of
\mathcal{C}
at all morphisms in
W
.

Definition 2.17
.

We denote by
\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)})^{\text{l-lax}}
the full
\infty
-subcategory of
(\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)})_{/[1]}
spanned by cocartesian fibrations, and by
\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)})^{\text{l-lax}}
the full
\infty
-subcategory of
(\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)})_{/[1]^{\mathrm{op}}}
spanned by cartesian fibrations. In both cases an object is given by a functor of
{(\infty,1)}
-categories
\mathcal{C}(0)\to\mathcal{C}(1)
; a morphism from
(\mathcal{C}(0)\to\mathcal{C}(1))
to
(\mathcal{D}(0)\to\mathcal{D}(1))
is also called a left/right lax natural transformation between functors
[1]\to\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)}
; it looks like a square of
{(\infty,1)}
-category commuting up to a natural transformation, whose orientation dictates the difference between the two cases:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}(0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}(0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}(1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}(1).}
l-lax
r-lax

We denote by
d_{0}
and
d_{1}
the functors
\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)})^{\text{l-lax}},\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)})^{\text{r-lax}}\to\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)}
taking fibres of cocartesian fibrations over
[1]
(respectively, cartesian fibrations over
[1]^{\mathrm{op}}
) at 1 (for
d_{0}
) and at 0 (for
d_{1}
).

Lemma 2.18
.

Let
q\colon\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Rel}\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)}
be a functor, and denote by
p_{j}\colon\mathcal{C}_{j}\to\mathcal{D}
the cartesian fibration corresponding to
d_{j}q
for
j=0,1
. Let
W=\mathcal{C}_{1}\times_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{D}^{\simeq}
, and consider
W\subset\mathcal{C}_{1}\subset\mathcal{C}_{0}
as a wide subcategory. Then the localised functor
L(\mathcal{C}_{0},W)\to\mathcal{D}
is a cartesian fibration, and its straightening is the composite
Lq
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{D}
denote a right fibration corresponding to
L\circ q\colon\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)}
, and let
\mathcal{E}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)}
. We then have equivalences of spaces

\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},\mathcal{E})^{\simeq}\simeq\mathrm{Fun}_{\mathcal{D}}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},\mathcal{E}\times\mathcal{D})^{\simeq}\simeq\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)})^{\text{l-lax}})^{\simeq}_{Lq,\mathrm{const}_{\mathcal{E}}},

where
\mathrm{Fun}_{\mathcal{D}}
denotes functors that are compatible with the projection to
\mathcal{D}
, but need not preserve cartesian morphisms; and
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)})^{\text{l-lax}})^{\simeq}_{Lq,\mathrm{const}_{\mathcal{E}}}
denotes the fibre over
(Lq,\mathrm{const}_{\mathcal{E}})
of the map of spaces

(d_{1},d_{0})\colon\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)})^{\text{l-lax}})^{\simeq}\to(\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)})^{\simeq})^{2}.

In other words, the last space is the space of left lax natural transformations from
Lq
to
\mathrm{const}_{\mathcal{E}}
.
Composition with the (strict) natural transformation
d_{0}q\to Lq
allows us to identify
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)})^{\text{l-lax}})^{\simeq}_{Lq,\mathrm{const}_{\mathcal{E}}}
with the subspace of
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)})^{\text{l-lax}})^{\simeq}_{d_{0}q,\mathrm{const}_{\mathcal{E}}}
comprising those left lax natural transformations that, for each
x\in\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{op}}
, restrict to a functor
d_{0}q(x)\to\mathcal{E}
which is
d_{1}q(x)
-local. And this subspace, under the composite equivalence of spaces

\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)})^{\text{l-lax}})^{\simeq}_{d_{0}q,\mathrm{const}_{\mathcal{E}}}\simeq\mathrm{Fun}_{\mathcal{D}}(\mathcal{C}_{0},\mathcal{E}\times\mathcal{D})^{\simeq}\simeq\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C}_{0},\mathcal{E})^{\simeq},

corresponds to the subspace of
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C}_{0},\mathcal{E})^{\simeq}
comprising
W
-local functors.
∎

Lemma 2.19
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},W)\in\mathrm{Rel}\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)}
, let
p\colon\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}
be a cartesian fibration, and assume that the functor
q\colon\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)}
corresponding to
p
is
W^{\mathrm{op}}
-local. Let
\mathrm{cart}_{W}\subset\mathcal{D}
denote the wide subcategory comprising all cartesian lifts of morphisms in
W
. Then the localised functor
L(\mathcal{D},\mathrm{cart}_{W})\to L(\mathcal{C},W)
is again a cartesian fibration, and it corresponds to the functor
L(\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}},W^{\mathrm{op}})\to\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)}
induced by
q
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}:=L(\mathcal{C},W)
, let

\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
be a cartesian fibration corresponding to
q\in\mathrm{Fun}((\mathcal{C}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)})^{\simeq}\subset\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)})^{\simeq}
, and let
\mathcal{E}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)}
. Then we have equivalences of spaces

\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime},\mathcal{E})^{\simeq}\simeq\mathrm{Fun}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime},\mathcal{E}\times\mathcal{C}^{\prime})^{\simeq}\simeq\mathrm{Fun}((\mathcal{C}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)})^{\text{l-lax}})^{\simeq}_{q,\mathrm{const}_{\mathcal{E}}}.

The localisation functor
\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{op}}

allows us to identify the last space with the subspace of
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)})^{\text{l-lax}})^{\simeq}_{q,\mathrm{const}_{\mathcal{E}}}
comprising
W
-local functors, and under the composite equivalence

\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)})^{\text{l-lax}})^{\simeq}_{q,\mathrm{const}_{\mathcal{E}}}\simeq\mathrm{Fun}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{E})\simeq\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{E}),

this corresponds to the subspace of
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{E})
comprising
\mathrm{cart}_{W}
-local functors.
∎

The following is an adaptation of
[
3
, Chapter 7.2]
for
left
calculi of fractions.

Definition 2.20
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},W)\in\mathrm{Rel}\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)}
and let
x\in\mathcal{C}
. A
left calculus of fractions at
x
is a functor
\pi(x)\colon W(x)\to\mathcal{C}_{x/}
satisfying the following properties:

•

the
{(\infty,1)}
-category
W(x)
admits an initial object
x_{0}
, and
\pi(x)\colon x_{0}\mapsto\mathrm{Id}_{x}
;

•

\pi(x)
takes value in the full subcategory of
\mathcal{C}_{x/}
spanned by arrows that are contained in
W
;

•

the left Kan extension functor
(d_{0}\pi(x))_{!}\colon\mathscr{P}(W(x))\to\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C})
sends the terminal presheaf
*\in\mathscr{P}(W(x))
to a presheaf
(d_{0}\pi(x))_{!}(*)\in\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C})
that factors through
L(\mathcal{C},W)
, i.e. morphisms in
W^{\mathrm{op}}
are sent to invertible morphisms in
\mathcal{S}
along
(d_{0}\pi(x))_{!}(*)
.

We remark that, for any functor
\pi(x)\colon W(x)\to\mathcal{C}_{x/}
and for
y\in\mathcal{C}
, we have
(d_{0}\pi(x))_{!}(*)(y)\simeq\mathrm{colim}_{z\in W(x)}\mathcal{C}(y,d_{0}\pi(z))
; we can also consider the category
\mathcal{C}_{y/}\times_{\mathcal{C}}W(x)
, where the fibre product is taken along the functors
d_{0}\colon\mathcal{C}_{y/}\to\mathcal{C}
and
d_{0}\pi\colon W(x)\to\mathcal{C}
; then we have
(d_{0}\pi(x))_{!}(*)(y)\simeq|\mathcal{C}_{y/}\times_{\mathcal{C}}W(x)|
.

Theorem 2.21

(
[
3
, Theorem 7.2.8]
)
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},W)
be a relative
\infty
-category, let
x\in\mathcal{C}
and let
\pi(x)\colon W(x)\to\mathcal{C}_{x/}
be a left calculus of fractions at
x
. Then we have an equivalence in
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C})

L(\mathcal{C},W)(-,x)\simeq(d_{0}\pi(x))_{!}(*).

3.
The
{(\infty,2)}
-category
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
of graph cobordisms between finite sets

In this section we introduce an (ordinary) symmetric monoidal 2-category of graph cobordisms between finite sets, denoted
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
; the main result of the next section will be a universal property of
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
as a symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,2)}
-category.

Roughly speaking, objects of
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
are finite sets; a 1-morphism between the finite sets
B
and
A
is the datum of a graph
G
attached to
A
and with a marking from
B
; and a 2-morphism between the 1-morphisms
B\overset{G}{\to}A
and
B\overset{G^{\prime}}{\to}A
is a map
G\to G^{\prime}
collapsing trees and compatible with the inclusions of
A
and the maps from
B
. Disjoint union of finite sets and graphs gives the symmetric monoidal structure.

Remark 3.1
.

Categories of graphs and graph collapses are classical objects. See for example
[
7
, §1.1]
and
[
4
, §2.1]
, where all maps between graphs are allowed (and not only those collapsing trees), and
[
4
, §1.1]
, where graphs with a marking by the standard rose, together with tree collapse maps, are considered.

3.1.
Marked graphs attached to a finite set

The following definition is a variation of the notion of combinatorial graph introduced by Gersten
[
7
]
.

Definition 3.2
.

A
marked graph attached to a finite set
(shortly “gaf”), is a sequence
G=(A,B,V,H,\rho,\sigma,\upsilon)
consisting of:

•

finite sets
A,B,V,H
of “attaching vertices”, “markings”, “inner vertices” and “half-edges”;

•

maps of finite sets
\rho,\sigma,\upsilon\colon A\sqcup B\sqcup V\sqcup H\to A\sqcup B\sqcup V\sqcup H
,

satisfying the following requirements:

•

\rho
restricts to the identity on
A\sqcup V\sqcup H
and to a map
B\to A\sqcup V
;

•

\sigma
and
\upsilon
restrict to the identity on
A\sqcup B\sqcup V
;

•

\sigma
restricts to a map
H\to A\sqcup V
.

•

\upsilon
is an involution acting freely on
H
.

We say that
G
is a graph
attached to
A
and
marked by
B
.
A sub-gaf of a gaf
G
as above is obtained by selecting subsets
A^{\prime}\subseteq A
,
B^{\prime}\subseteq B
,
V^{\prime}\subseteq V
and
H^{\prime}\subseteq H
such that the maps
\rho,\sigma,\upsilon
restrict to self-maps of
A^{\prime}\sqcup B^{\prime}\sqcup V^{\prime}\sqcup H^{\prime}
.

Notation 3.3
.

We usually expand a gaf
G
as
(A,B,V,H,\rho,\sigma,\upsilon)
, we expand a gaf
G^{\prime}
as
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime},V^{\prime},H^{\prime},\rho^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime},\upsilon^{\prime})
, and so on.
We usually define sub-gaf’s of a gaf
G
by declaring a (suitable) subset of
A\sqcup B\sqcup V\sqcup H
.

For a gaf
G
, we usually denote by
E=H/\upsilon
the set of
\upsilon
-orbits contained in
H
, and refer to elements in
E
as “edges” of
G
. Similarly, we write
E^{\prime}=H^{\prime}/\upsilon^{\prime}
for the set of edges of
G^{\prime}
, and so on.

Definition 3.4
.

Given a gaf
G
, we let
\Re(G)
denote the 1-dimensional cell complex having a 0-cell for each element in
A\sqcup V
and a 1-cell for each element in
E
. The two attaching points of the 1-cell corresponding to
e=\left\{h_{1},h_{2}\right\}\subseteq H
are the (possibly equal) 0-cells
\sigma(h_{1})
and
\sigma(h_{2})
.

Definition
3.4
explains the use of the word “graph” in Definition
3.2
.
A morphism of gaf’s shall be required to be compatible with attaching vertices and markings, and to induce at the level of cell complexes a “nice” homotopy equivalence. This is achieved by the following combinatorial implementation.

Definition 3.5
.

A gaf
G
is a
based tree
(respectively,
a
non-based tree
) if
\Re(G)
is contractible, and moreover
|A|=1
(respectively,
A=\emptyset
).

Definition 3.6
.

A
morphism of gaf’s

f\colon G\to G^{\prime}
is a map of sets
f\colon A\sqcup B\sqcup V\sqcup H\to A^{\prime}\sqcup B^{\prime}\sqcup V^{\prime}\sqcup H^{\prime}
satisfying the following conditions:

(1)

f
restricts to maps
A\to A^{\prime}
and
B\to B^{\prime}
;

(2)

f\circ\rho=\rho^{\prime}\circ f
,
f\circ\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}\circ f
and
f\circ\upsilon=\upsilon^{\prime}\circ f
; in particular
f
restricts to maps
V\to A\sqcup V^{\prime}
and
H\to A^{\prime}\sqcup V^{\prime}\sqcup H^{\prime}
;

(3)

for all
v^{\prime}\in V^{\prime}
,
f^{-1}(v^{\prime})
is a non-based subtree of
G
;

(4)

for all
a^{\prime}\in A^{\prime}
,
f^{-1}(a^{\prime})
is a disjoint union of based subtrees of
G
, one for each element in
f^{-1}(a^{\prime})\cap A
.

(5)

for all
h^{\prime}\in H^{\prime}
,
f^{-1}(h^{\prime})
is a singleton.

We let
\mathrm{Gaf}
denote the 1-category of gaf’s and morphisms of gaf’s. We consider
\mathrm{Gaf}
as a symmetric monoidal category by disjoint union of finite sets and graphs.

We denote by
\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{B}\colon\mathrm{Gaf}\to\mathrm{Fin}
the symmetric monoidal functors given by
\mathfrak{A}(G)=A
and
\mathfrak{B}(G)=B
, respectively.

3.2.
Graph cobordisms between finite sets

Definition 3.7
.

Let
A
and
B
be finite sets. We denote by
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(B,A)
the category obtained as fibre over the object
(B,A)
of the functor
\mathfrak{B}\times\mathfrak{A}\colon\mathrm{Gaf}\to\mathrm{Fin}\times\mathrm{Fin}
. This is the subcategory of
\mathrm{Gaf}
whose objects are those gaf’s attached to
A
and marked by
B
, and whose morphisms restrict to the identities of
A
and
B
.

Example 3.8
.

For
A=B=\emptyset
, the category
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\emptyset,\emptyset)
admits a symmetric monoidal structure by disjoint union, and it is symmetric monoidally freely generated by its full subcategory
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\emptyset,\emptyset)^{\mathrm{conn}}
spanned by gaf’s
G
with
\Re(G)
connected and non-empty. In turn,
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\emptyset,\emptyset)^{\mathrm{conn}}
is the disjoint union of its full subcategories
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\emptyset,\emptyset)^{\mathrm{conn}}_{n}
, for
n\geq 0
, spanned by non-empty connected graphs of rank
n
.

As we will see in Theorem
8.4
, the classifying space of the category
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\emptyset,\emptyset)^{\mathrm{conn}}_{n}

is an Eilenberg-MacLane space of type
K(\mathrm{Out}(F_{n}),1)
, where

\mathrm{Out}(F_{n})
denote the group of outer automorphisms of a free group of rank
n
.

Given finite sets
A,A^{\prime},A^{\prime\prime}
, we construct a functor
\circ_{\mathrm{h}}\colon{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(A^{\prime},A)\times{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(A^{\prime\prime},A^{\prime})\to{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(A^{\prime\prime},A)
by
glueing graphs along
A^{\prime}
as follows. Let
G^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(A^{\prime\prime},A^{\prime})
and
G\in{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(A^{\prime},A)
; by Notation
3.3
, we have in particular
B^{\prime}=A^{\prime\prime}
and
B=A^{\prime}
. We let
G\circ_{\mathrm{h}}G^{\prime}
be the gaf
\hat{G}
with
\hat{A}=A
,
\hat{B}=B^{\prime}=A^{\prime\prime}
,
\hat{V}=V\sqcup V^{\prime}
,
\hat{H}=H\sqcup H^{\prime}
and whose structure maps
\hat{\rho}
,
\hat{\sigma}
and
\hat{\upsilon}
characterised by the following:

•

\hat{\rho}|_{A^{\prime\prime}}
is the composite of
\rho^{\prime}\colon B^{\prime}\to A^{\prime}\sqcup V^{\prime}=B\sqcup V^{\prime}
and
(\rho\sqcup\mathrm{Id}_{V})\colon B\sqcup V^{\prime}\to A\sqcup V\sqcup V^{\prime}=A\sqcup\hat{V}
;

•

\hat{\sigma}|_{H}=\sigma
, and
\hat{\sigma}|_{H^{\prime}}=(\rho\sqcup\mathrm{Id}_{V^{\prime}})\circ\sigma^{\prime}
.

•

\hat{\upsilon}|_{\hat{H}}=\upsilon\sqcup\upsilon^{\prime}
;

The behaviour of
\circ_{\mathrm{h}}
on morphisms is similar: given morphisms
f^{\prime}\colon G^{\prime}\to G^{\prime}_{1}
in
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(A^{\prime\prime},A^{\prime})
and
f\colon G\to G_{1}
in
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(A^{\prime},A)
, we let
f\circ_{\mathrm{h}}f^{\prime}
be the map of gaf’s
\hat{f}\colon\hat{G}\to\hat{G}_{1}
agreeing with
f
and
f^{\prime}
on the different finite sets defining
\hat{G}
, where we postcompose with
\rho_{1}
whenever
f^{\prime}
lands in
A_{1}^{\prime}
. Among the conditions of Definition
3.6
, the less obvious to check for
\hat{f}
are (3) and (4). For this, we observe the following:

•

for
v^{\prime}_{1}\in V^{\prime}_{1}\subset\hat{V}_{1}
,
\hat{f}^{-1}(v^{\prime}_{1})
is isomorphic to the non-based tree
(f^{\prime})^{-1}(v^{\prime}_{1})
;

•

for
v_{1}\in V_{1}\subset\hat{V}_{1}
,
\hat{f}^{-1}(v_{1})
is isomorphic to the gaf obtained by glueing the non-based tree
f^{-1}(v_{1})
with the based trees
(f^{\prime})^{-1}(a^{\prime}_{1})
, for
a^{\prime}_{1}
ranging in
\rho_{1}^{-1}(v_{1})\subset A^{\prime}_{1}
: we glue the unique vertex
a^{\prime}\in(f^{\prime})^{-1}(a^{\prime}_{1})\cap A^{\prime}
to the vertex
\rho(a^{\prime})\in f^{-1}(v)
; the result is a non-based tree;

•

for
a\in A
, the situation is analogous to the previous one, except that the result is a based tree, as
f^{-1}(a)
is now a based tree.

The functors
\circ_{\mathrm{h}}
satisfy associativity up to natural equivalence, coherently for iterated compositions. We obtain therefore a 2-category
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
, whose objects are finite sets, and whose morphism 1-category from
B
to
A
is
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(B,A)
. Horizontal composition of morphisms is given by the functors
\circ_{\mathrm{h}}
. For each finite set
A
, the gaf
(A,A,\emptyset,\emptyset,\mathrm{Id}_{A},\emptyset,\emptyset)\in{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(A,A)
is an identity object in
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(A,A)
, where “
\emptyset
” denotes the empty set or the empty map.

We also want to enhance
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
to a
symmetric monoidal
2-category, with monoidal product given by taking disjoint unions of finite sets, gaf’s, and maps of gaf’s. To do this formally, we first define for a finite pointed set
S_{*}=S\sqcup\left\{*\right\}
a variation
\mathrm{Gaf}_{S}
of
\mathrm{Gaf}
: an object in
\mathrm{Gaf}_{S}
is a tuple
(A,B,V,H,\rho,\sigma,\upsilon)
of objects and morphisms in
\mathrm{Fin}_{/S}
, whose image along
d_{1}\colon\mathrm{Fin}_{/S}\to\mathrm{Fin}
satisfies the requirements of Definition
3.2
; a morphism in
\mathrm{Gaf}_{S}
is similarly a morphism in
\mathrm{Fin}_{/S}
whose image along
d_{1}
satisfies the requirements from Definition
3.6
. The assignment
S_{*}\mapsto{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}_{S}
extends to a functor
\mathrm{Fin}_{*}\to\mathrm{Cat}_{2}
: given a map of finite pointed sets
g\colon S_{*}\to S_{*}^{\prime}
, we consider the functor
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}_{S}\to{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}_{S^{\prime}}
that forgets all data lying over
g^{-1}(*)
, and composes the remaining data with the restricted map
g\colon g^{-1}(S^{\prime})\to S^{\prime}
.

The constructed functor
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}_{(-)}\mathrm{Fin}_{*}\to\mathrm{Cat}_{2}
satisfies the Segal condition: for
S_{*}\in\mathrm{Fin}_{*}
we have an equivalence
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}_{S}\xrightarrow{\simeq}{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}^{S}
induced by the
S
maps
S_{*}\to\underline{1}_{*}
in
\mathrm{Fin}_{*}
that send a single element of
S
to
\underline{1}
, and the rest to
*
. A functor
\mathrm{Fin}_{*}\to\mathrm{Cat}_{2}
satisfying the Segal condition shall be our notion of symmetric monoidal 2-category.

Remark 3.9
.

The lack of strictness for
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
is due to the fact that, in defining
\circ_{\mathrm{h}}
, we have chosen a disjoint union functor
-\sqcup-\colon\mathrm{Fin}\times\mathrm{Fin}\to\mathrm{Fin}
. One can in fact give a
strict
2-category equivalent to
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
by requiring all finite sets
V,H,E
appearing in the description of an object in
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(B,A)
to be initial segments
\underline{k}
of
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, and by using concatenation of initial segments of
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
to implement disjoint union. However, even with this model,
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
will not be strict as a (symmetric)
monoidal
2-category: given pairs of composable 1-morphisms
(G_{1},G_{2})
and
(G^{\prime}_{1},G^{\prime}_{2})
, the 1-morphisms
(G_{1}\sqcup G^{\prime}_{1})\circ_{\mathrm{h}}(G_{2}\sqcup G^{\prime}_{2})
and
(G_{1}\circ_{\mathrm{h}}G_{2})\sqcup(G^{\prime}_{1}\circ_{\mathrm{h}}G^{\prime}_{2})
will be connected by a canonical 2-isomorphism which is in general not an identity. This problem is unavoidable: the braided monoidal category
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\emptyset,\emptyset)
, of endomorphisms of the monoidal unit, has a non-trivial braiding
G\sqcup G\simeq G\sqcup G
for all non-empty gafs
G\in{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\emptyset,\emptyset)
, contradicting the Eckmann–Hilton argument that would hold in a strict monoidal 2-category.

We will mainly consider
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
as a symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,2)}
-category, and we will call it the
{(\infty,2)}
-category of
graph cobordisms between finite sets
.

Notation 3.10
.

We let
\mathrm{Gr}\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)})
denote the symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,1)}
-category
|{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}|_{2}
obtained from
\mathrm{Gr}
by inverting all 2-morphisms.

Remark 3.11
.

Galatius
[
5
, Section 4]
introduces a category of graph cobordisms between finite sets, denoted
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
: it is a category internal to
\mathrm{Top}
. Jan Steinebrunner pointed out to us that the underlying
{(\infty,1)}
-category of
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
is
not
equivalent to
\mathrm{Gr}
(though it is likely equivalent to a wide
{(\infty,1)}
-subcategory of
\mathrm{Gr}
): indeed, Galatius only considers graphs whose vertices have valence at least 3, hence
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
cannot account for graph cobordisms having components homotopy equivalent to a point or a circle that are not attached to the target finite set.
Jan Steinebrunner has also pointed out to us that the combination of Propositions 4.8 and 4.9, and Theorem 5.1 in
[
5
]
, together with Corollary
A.2
, prove at least the equivalence
|\mathcal{C}_{\infty}|\simeq\Omega^{\infty-1}\mathbb{S}\simeq|{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}|
; in this sense, the first statement akin to Corollary
A.2
appearing in the literature is due to Galatius. Compare also with
[
13
, Remark 4.18]
.

3.3.
Some properties of
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}

We next highlight some features of
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
.

Notation 3.12
.

We denote by
\mu\colon\underline{2}\to\underline{1}
be the unique map of finite sets.

The functor
\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
:

Consider
\mathrm{Fin}
as a symmetric monoidal 2-category having only identity 2-morphisms; then we have a symmetric monoidal 2-functor
\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}\colon\mathrm{Fin}\to{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
, sending
A\mapsto A
and sending a map of finite sets
f\colon B\to A
to the unique gaf
G\in{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(B,A)
with
V=H=\emptyset
and
\rho=f
.

The 1-morphism
\mathfrak{t}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
:

We have a special 1-morphism
\mathfrak{t}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}\colon\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\underline{1})\to\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\underline{0})
, given by the unique gaf
G_{\mathfrak{t}}\in{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\underline{1},\underline{0})
with
V_{\mathfrak{t}}=\underline{1}
. We represent
\mathfrak{t}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
pictorially as follows:
1
\bullet
\bullet
\mathfrak{t}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
\emptyset

The
C_{2}
-equivariant 1-morphism
\mathfrak{e}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
:

We have in
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
a special 1-morphism
\mathfrak{e}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}\colon\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\underline{0})\to\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\underline{2})
, given by the unique gaf
G_{\mathfrak{e}}\in{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\underline{0},\underline{2})
with
V_{\mathfrak{e}}=\emptyset
,
H_{\mathfrak{e}}=\underline{2}
and
\sigma_{\mathfrak{e}}=\mathrm{Id}_{\underline{2}}
. We represent
\mathfrak{e}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
pictorially as follows:
\emptyset
\bullet
1
\bullet
2
\mathfrak{e}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}

We further consider the trivial and free action of the group
C_{2}
on the objects
\underline{0}
and
\underline{2}
of
\mathrm{Fin}
, respectively, and obtain an action of
C_{2}
on the objects
\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\underline{0})
and
\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\underline{2})
of
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
and, by conjugation, on the category
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\underline{0}),\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\underline{2}))
. Then
G_{\mathfrak{e}}\in{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\underline{0}),\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\underline{2}))
admits a natural
C_{2}
-equivariant structure, induced by the unique non-trivial involution
f\colon G_{\mathfrak{e}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}G_{\mathfrak{e}}
in
\mathrm{Gaf}
acting freely on both
A=H_{\mathfrak{e}}=\underline{2}
.

The 2-morphism
\beta_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
:

Using the above, we can construct the following 1-morphisms
G_{\beta,1},G_{\beta,2}\colon\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\underline{1})\to\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\underline{1})
in
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
. We let

G_{\beta,1}=((\mathfrak{t}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}\circ_{\mathrm{h}}\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\mu))\sqcup\mathrm{Id}_{\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\underline{1})})\circ_{\mathrm{h}}(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\underline{1})}\sqcup\mathfrak{e}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}),

i.e. the following horizontal composite, where we remove all instances of “
\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{1}\cong\underline{1}\sqcup\underline{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{1}\sqcup\underline{2}\cong\underline{3}\cong\underline{2}\sqcup\underline{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{0}\sqcup\underline{1}\cong\underline{1}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Id}_{\underline{1}}\sqcup\mathfrak{e}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathfrak{t}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}\circ_{\mathrm{h}}\mu)\sqcup\mathrm{Id}_{\underline{1}}}

We let
G_{\beta,2}
be the identity 1-morphism
\mathrm{Id}_{\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\underline{1})}
. Then we have a special 2-morphism
\beta_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}\colon G_{\beta,1}\Rightarrow G_{\beta,2}
, which we describe in the following. As gaf’s,
G_{\beta,1}
is characterised up to unique isomorphism by the following:
A_{\beta,1}=B_{\beta,1}=\underline{1}
and
V_{\beta,1}=\left\{v\right\}\simeq\underline{1}
are singletons;
H_{\beta,1}=\left\{h_{1},h_{v}\right\}\simeq\underline{2}
has two elements;
\sigma_{\beta,1}
sends
h_{1}\mapsto 1\in A_{\beta,1}
and
h_{v}\mapsto v\in V_{\beta,1}
; and
\rho_{\beta,1}
sends
1\mapsto v
. We similarly characterise
G_{\beta,2}
by
A_{\beta,2}=B_{\beta,2}=\underline{1}
and
V_{\beta,2}=H_{\beta,2}=\emptyset
. We then define
\beta_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}\colon G_{\beta,1}\to G_{\beta,2}
as the unique morphism of gaf’s, sending

V_{\beta,1}\sqcup H_{\beta,1}
constantly to
1\in A_{\beta,2}
. At the level of graphs,
G_{\beta,1}
is a segment with one attaching vertex and marked once at the other vertex, whereas
G_{\beta,2}
consists of a single once-marked attaching vertex; then
\beta_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
is the contraction of
G_{\beta,1}
onto
G_{\beta,2}
.
Pictorially,
\beta_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
straightens the above S-shaped composition giving
G_{\beta,1}
to a straight segment.

The
C_{2}
-equivariant 2-morphism
\tilde{\beta}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}

Using the above, there is a canonical identification of the following two 1-morphisms in
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\underline{2}),\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\underline{0}))

\begin{array}[]{c}\mathfrak{t}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}\circ_{\mathrm{h}}\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\mu)\circ_{\mathrm{h}}(G_{\beta,1}\sqcup\mathrm{Id}_{\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\underline{1})});\\[2.84544pt]
(\mathfrak{t}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}\sqcup\mathfrak{t}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}})\circ_{\mathrm{h}}(\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\mu)\sqcup\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\mu))\circ_{\mathrm{h}}(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\underline{1})}\sqcup\mathfrak{e}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}\sqcup\mathrm{Id}_{\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\underline{1})}),\end{array}

given by replacing “

. Both of the following represent pictorially

where we write identities of 1-morphisms as the 1-morphisms themselves.

We have actions of
C_{2}
on
\underline{2}
(by swap), on
\underline{1}
(trivial action) and on
\underline{4}
(by the map exchanging 1-4 and 2-3). The horizontal factors of the second formula for

, namely
(\mathfrak{t}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}\sqcup\mathfrak{t}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}})\in{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\underline{2}),\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\underline{0}))
,
(\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\mu)\sqcup\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\mu))\in{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\underline{4}),\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\underline{2}))
and
(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\underline{1})}\sqcup\mathfrak{e}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}\sqcup\mathrm{Id}_{\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\underline{1})})\in{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\underline{2}),\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\underline{4}))
have a natural structure of
C_{2}
-equivariant objects in the corresponding
C_{2}
-equivariant categories: the last one, for instance, is obtained combining the
C_{2}
-equivariant structure on
\mathfrak{e}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
and symmetric monoidality of

. Similarly,
G_{\tilde{\beta},2}
has a

-equivariant structure in
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\underline{2}),\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\underline{0}))
. It makes thus sense to ask whether or not
\tilde{\beta}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
is a
C_{2}
-equivariant morphism
1
1

1

Note that
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\underline{2},\underline{0})
is a
plain 1-category
with
C_{2}
-action, so even if “being
C_{2}
-equivariant” is a structure on an object, it is a property for a morphism between
C_{2}
-equivariant objects.
. At the level of graphs,
\tilde{\beta}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
is the contraction of the segment
G_{\tilde{\beta},1}
onto the point
G_{\tilde{\beta},2}
, and this is clearly invariant under swapping the two endpoints of
G_{\tilde{\beta},1}
, together with their markings, and also swapping the two half-edges of
G_{\tilde{\beta},1}
. So
\tilde{\beta}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
is indeed
C_{2}
-equivariant.

Passing to
{(\infty,2)}
-categories, we have the following structure on
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
:

•

{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
is a symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,2)}
-category;

•

we are given a symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,2)}
-functor
\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}\colon\mathrm{Fin}\to{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
;

•

we are given a 1-morphism
\mathfrak{t}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}\colon\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\underline{1})\to\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\underline{0})
;

•

we are given a 1-morphism
\mathfrak{e}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}\colon\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\underline{0})\to\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\underline{2})
, and a
C_{2}
-equivariant structure on
\mathfrak{e}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
as an object of the
C_{2}
-equivariant
{(\infty,1)}
-category

{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\underline{0}),\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\underline{2}));

•

we are given a 2-morphism

\beta_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}\colon((\mathfrak{t}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}\circ_{\mathrm{h}}\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\mu))\sqcup\mathrm{Id}_{\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\underline{1})})\circ_{\mathrm{h}}(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\underline{1})}\sqcup\mathfrak{e}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}})\Rightarrow\mathrm{Id}_{\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\underline{1})};

•

we are given a
C_{2}
-equivariant structure on the resulting 2-morphism

\tilde{\beta}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}:=\mathfrak{t}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}\circ_{\mathrm{h}}\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\mu)\circ_{\mathrm{h}}(\beta_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}\sqcup\mathrm{Id}_{\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\underline{1})}),

considered as a morphism between
C_{2}
-equivariant objects in the
C_{2}
-equivariant
{(\infty,1)}
-category
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\underline{2}),\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}(\underline{0}))
.

3.4.
Graph-like structures

We axiomatise the previous discussion about
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
.

Definition 3.13
.

A
semi-graph-like structure
on a symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,2)}
-category
\mathcal{D}
is the datum of:

•

a symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,2)}
-functor
\mathfrak{I}\colon\mathrm{Fin}\to\mathcal{D}
;

•

an object
\mathfrak{t}
in the
{(\infty,1)}
-category
\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{I}(\underline{1}),\mathfrak{I}(\underline{0}))
;

•

a
C_{2}
-equivariant object
\mathfrak{e}
in
\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{I}(\underline{0}),\mathfrak{I}(\underline{2}))
, which is a
C_{2}
-equivariant
{(\infty,1)}
-category.

A
graph-like structure
on
\mathcal{D}
is the datum of a semi-graph-like structure as above, together with:

•

a 2-morphism
\beta\colon((\mathfrak{t}\circ_{\mathrm{h}}\mathfrak{I}(\mu))\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{\mathfrak{I}(\underline{1})})\circ_{\mathrm{h}}(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathfrak{I}(\underline{1})}\otimes\mathfrak{e})\Rightarrow\mathrm{Id}_{\mathfrak{I}(\underline{1})}
, which can be regarded as a morphism in the
{(\infty,1)}
-category
\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{I}(\underline{1}),\mathfrak{I}(\underline{1}))
;

•

a
C_{2}
-equivariant structure on the 2-morphism
\tilde{\beta}:=\mathfrak{t}\circ_{\mathrm{h}}\mathfrak{I}(\mu)\circ_{\mathrm{h}}(\beta\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{\mathfrak{I}(\underline{1})})
, which can be regarded as a morphism in the
C_{2}
-equivariant
{(\infty,1)}
-category
\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{I}(\underline{2}),\mathfrak{I}(\underline{0}))
between
C_{2}
-equivariant objects.

Notation 3.14
.

We usually denote a graph-like structure as in Definition
3.13
by
(\mathfrak{I},\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{e},\beta)
, leaving the other data, especially the various
C_{2}
-equivariant structures, understood.
For instance, the graph-like structure on
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
from Subsection
3.3
will be denoted
(\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}},\mathfrak{t}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}},\mathfrak{e}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}},\beta_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}})
.

In the following we construct functors
\mathbf{SGL},\mathbf{GL}\colon\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)})\to\mathcal{S}
associating with a symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,2)}
-category
\mathcal{D}
its
spaces
of semi-graph-like and graph-like structures, respectively.

Definition 3.15
.

We denote by
\mathbf{SGL}
the limit of the following W-shaped diagram of limit-preserving and accessible functors
\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)})\to\mathcal{S}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}([1],-)^{\simeq}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}^{\otimes}(\mathrm{Fin},-)^{\simeq}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{B}C_{2}\times[1],-)^{\simeq}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(\left\{0,1\right\},-)^{\simeq}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{B}C_{2}\times\left\{0,1\right\},-)^{\simeq}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(d_{1},d_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ev}_{\underline{0}}\times\mathrm{ev}_{\underline{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ev}_{\underline{1}}\times\mathrm{ev}_{\underline{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(d_{1},d_{0})}

Informally, for
\mathcal{D}\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)})
,
\mathbf{SGL}(\mathcal{D})
is the space of choices
(\mathfrak{I},\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{e})
of a symmetric monoidal functor
\mathfrak{I}\colon\mathrm{Fin}\to\mathcal{D}
, an object
\mathfrak{t}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{I}(\underline{1}),\mathfrak{I}(\underline{0}))
, and a
C_{2}
-equivariant object
\mathfrak{e}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{I}(\underline{0}),\mathfrak{I}(\underline{2}))
.
We have a natural transformation
\mathbf{SGL}\Rightarrow\mathrm{Fun}(\mathrm{O}_{1},-)^{\simeq}
of functors
\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)})\to\mathcal{S}
, sending
(\mathfrak{I},\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{e})\in\mathbf{SGL}(\mathcal{D})
to the following
\mathrm{O}_{1}
-shaped diagram in
\mathcal{D}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{I}(\underline{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{I}(\underline{1}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((\mathfrak{t}\circ_{\mathrm{h}}\mathfrak{I}(\mu))\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{\mathfrak{I}(\underline{1})})\circ_{\mathrm{h}}(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathfrak{I}(\underline{1})}\otimes\mathfrak{e})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Id}_{\mathfrak{I}(\underline{1})}}

We then let
\mathbf{SGL}^{\prime}\colon\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)})\to\mathcal{S}
be pullback of the cospan diagram of functors
\mathbf{SGL}\to\mathrm{Fun}(\mathrm{O}_{1},-)^{\simeq}\leftarrow\mathrm{Fun}(\Theta_{2},-)^{\simeq}
: again
\mathbf{SGL}^{\prime}
is limit-preserving and accessible; the space
\mathbf{SGL}^{\prime}(\mathcal{D})
parametrises choices of a sequence
(\mathfrak{I},\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{e},\beta)
as in Definition
3.13
, with a
C_{2}
-equivariant structure on
\mathfrak{e}
. Finally, we construct a natural transformation
\mathbf{SGL}^{\prime}\Rightarrow\mathrm{Fun}((\mathbf{B}C_{2}\times\mathrm{O}_{1})\sqcup_{(*\times\mathrm{O}_{1})}(*\times\Theta_{2}),-)^{\simeq}
, sending
(\mathfrak{I},\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{e},\beta)\in\mathbf{SGL}^{\prime}(\mathcal{D})
to the following
\Theta_{2}
-diagram in
\mathcal{D}
, whose sub-
\mathrm{O}_{1}
-diagram is endowed with a
C_{2}
-equivariant structure

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{I}(\underline{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{I}(\underline{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{t}\circ_{\mathrm{h}}\mathfrak{I}(\mu)\circ_{\mathrm{h}}((((\mathfrak{t}\circ_{\mathrm{h}}\mathfrak{I}(\mu))\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{\mathfrak{I}(\underline{1})})\circ_{\mathrm{h}}(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathfrak{I}(\underline{1})}\otimes\mathfrak{e}))\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{\mathfrak{I}(\underline{1})})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{t}\circ_{\mathrm{h}}\mathfrak{I}(\mu)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\beta}:=\mathfrak{t}\circ_{\mathrm{h}}\mathfrak{I}(\mu)\circ_{\mathrm{h}}(\beta\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{\mathfrak{I}(\underline{1})})}

Definition 3.16
.

We define
\mathbf{GL}\colon\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)})\to\mathcal{S}
as the pullback of the cospan diagram of functors

\mathbf{SGL}^{\prime}\to\mathrm{Fun}\big((\mathbf{B}C_{2}\times\mathrm{O}_{1})\sqcup_{(*\times\mathrm{O}_{1})}(*\times\Theta_{2}),-\big)^{\simeq}\leftarrow\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{B}C_{2}\times\Theta_{2},-)^{\simeq}.

Again,
\mathbf{GL}
is limit-preserving and accessible;
the space
\mathbf{GL}(\mathcal{D})
is by definition the space of graph-like structure on
\mathcal{D}
.

Since the functors
\mathbf{SGL}
and
\mathbf{GL}
are limit-preserving and accessible functors between the presentable
{(\infty,1)}
-categories
\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)})
and
\mathcal{S}
, we can invoke
[
9
, Proposition 5.5.2.7]
and conclude that
\mathbf{SGL}
and
\mathbf{GL}
are representable.

Notation 3.17
.

We denote by
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{V}
the symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,2)}
-categories representing
\mathbf{SGL}
and
\mathbf{GL}
, respectively, i.e. we have equivalences
\mathrm{Fun}^{\otimes}(\mathcal{U},-)^{\simeq}\simeq\mathbf{SGL}
and
\mathrm{Fun}^{\otimes}(\mathcal{V},-)^{\simeq}\simeq\mathbf{GL}
.
We denote by
(\mathfrak{I}_{\mathcal{U}},\mathfrak{t}_{\mathcal{U}},\mathfrak{e}_{\mathcal{U}})
the universal semi-graph-like structure on
\mathcal{U}
, corresponding to
\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{U}}\in\mathrm{Fun}^{\otimes}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{U})
; similarly,
(\mathfrak{I}_{\mathcal{V}},\mathfrak{t}_{\mathcal{V}},\mathfrak{e}_{\mathcal{V}},\beta_{\mathcal{V}})
denotes the universal graph-like structure on
\mathcal{V}
.
Finally, we denote by
\mathfrak{J}\colon\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{V}
the symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,2)}
-functor corresponding to
(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{V}},\mathfrak{t}_{\mathcal{V}},\mathfrak{e}_{\mathcal{V}})
, and by
\mathfrak{G}\colon\mathcal{V}\to{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
the symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,2)}
-functor corresponding to
(\mathfrak{I}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}},\mathfrak{t}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}},\mathfrak{e}_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}},\beta_{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}})
.

Remark 3.18
.

We observe that
\mathbf{SGL}
factors through
(-)^{2\simeq}\colon\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}\to\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,1)}
, or in other words, that for any
\mathcal{D}\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)})
the inclusion
\mathcal{D}^{2\simeq}\to\mathcal{D}
induces an equivalence
\mathbf{SGL}(\mathcal{D}^{2\simeq})\simeq\mathbf{SGL}(\mathcal{D})
: indeed the sources
\star
of the functors
\mathrm{Fun}(\star,-)^{\simeq}
and
\mathrm{Fun}^{\otimes}(\star,-)
in the diagram of Definition
3.15
are all
{(\infty,1)}
-categories. As a consequence we have that
\mathcal{U}
is an
{(\infty,1)}
-category, i.e. all of its 2-morphisms are invertible, and
\mathfrak{J}
restricts to a symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,1)}
-functor
\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{V}^{2\simeq}
. We will see after Corollary
4.23
that the latter is in fact an equivalence.

We can now rephrase Theorem
A
as the statement that
\mathfrak{G}\colon\mathcal{V}\to{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}
is an equivalence of
{(\infty,2)}
-categories; we will prove this statement in
Section
7
.

A key step in proving Theorem
A
will be the following theorem, implying that
{\mathbb{G}\mathrm{r}}^{2\simeq}
represents
\mathbf{SGL}
.

Theorem 3.19
.

The symmetric monoidal
{(\infty,1)}
-functor
